x86: Intel Processor Trace. The processor records branch outcomes and, at selected branches, cycle counts. A decoder combines these records with the binaries to reconstruct the executed instructions. Only branches with cycle counts provide timing. Tracing inserts no instructions, but traced runs took 1.2% to 1.6% longer than untraced runs. The table reports medians to limit the effect of occasional slow samples.

StepWhat runsInstructionsCyclesTime (ns)
1Linux’s entry, up to the check of the syscall work bits644212.7
2The work bit, the call of the hook, and the hook’s first checks1892.7
3The hook’s last checks, and the copy from pt_regs to UserContext43154.5
4klet_enter: the run state, the mirror, the watch timer, and klet_switch saving and loading registers65247.3
5From klet_switch’s return to user_run’s return on the kernelet stack3164.9
6aThe kernel proper tells what the run returned10164.8
6bThe handler stores 123, execute sets the user flags and calls RawUserContext::run5257.4
7aRawUserContext::run’s checks: the current task, overflow checks, held spin locks2661.7
7bTwo dependent loads of the service table, and the jump to user_run3247.3
8user_run: publishing KLET_USER, the barrier lock addl, the pending check, and taking the mirror off24288.4
9klet_switch back onto the Linux stack, and klet_enter’s return2372.1
10aThe hook calls klet_space_adopt, which calls klet_space_get26165.0
10block cmpxchg on the space’s reference count5329.7
10cThe liveness check, the mm comparison, the call of klet_space_put1520.6
10dlock decl on the same count1237.0
10eThe returns to the hook620.6
11The hook’s check after the run, and the copy from UserContext back to pt_regs52154.5
12aLinux’s exit work63195.8
12bRestoring the user registers, and SYSRET235215.8
—User mode, from SYSRET to the next syscall13.3
Total475373126.1

The step medians sum to 126.1 ns. Without tracing, we measured 125.9–128.4 ns per call in the same boots.

The Linux–kernelet handoff costs 78.5 ns (steps 2–11). This proposal addresses the handoff overhead caused by differences between Linux’s and the kernelet’s execution models.

Two kernels’ models meet at the handoff

Linux gives each task a kernel stack and saves user registers there in pt_regs. No kernel call remains suspended while the task runs in user mode. In the prototype, kernelet tasks share a carrier’s Linux stack. Each task keeps execute suspended on its own kernelet stack and stores user registers in a separate UserContext. Each syscall copies registers between these representations and resumes the suspended call chain to reach the kernel proper.

The resumption steps take 9.7 ns; register copying and adjacent hook checks take about 9 ns. These two groups of steps total about 18.7 ns. They do not isolate the full cost of adapting the two execution models.

A Linux thread (a) and the prototype’s carrier (b) during user execution. Red numbers mark the two costs below: resuming calls (1) and copying registers (2).

  1. Resuming the suspended call: steps 5 and 6a, 9.7 ns. Each system call resumes user_run on the kernelet stack. Control returns through execute to the kernel proper, which checks the return reason (Appendix, execute and Two stacks). These steps execute 13 instructions. The stack switch itself costs little.
  2. Copying the registers: steps 3 and 11, about 9 ns. The hook copies 18 registers from Linux’s pt_regs to the kernelet’s UserContext, then copies them back before returning to user mode. These copies and the hook’s checks execute 95 instructions in about 30 cycles.

Other costs and engineering optimizations

  • Address-space checks (step 10, 22.9 ns). On each return, the prototype looks up the requested address space, takes and drops a reference, and compares it with the current space. The benchmark has one task, so its address space does not change. This design moves the switch to vm_activate and defers cleanup that can sleep, removing this work from the return path.
  • Event ordering (part of step 8). user_run publishes KLET_USER, then checks for pending virtual interrupts. A full memory barrier (lock addl) orders these steps. This design sets Linux’s notification flag for each virtual interrupt in any carrier state, removing the check and barrier from user_run.
  • Build settings (steps 6b and 7a). The prototype builds the image as a shared library. Calls between crates then go through the global offset table, and integer-overflow checks stay on. The release-lto profile makes a position-independent executable with direct calls and no overflow checks.
  • Service-table call (step 7b, 7.3 ns). The image permits only relative relocations and no imported symbols (The endovisor UAPI). The kernelet therefore calls user_run through a service table, with two dependent loads and an indirect jump. This design keeps this call.
  • The mirror and watch timer. This design keeps both; their optimization is outside its scope.

Proposed change

Design overview

One system call through the prototype (a) and this design (b). Steps on the same row do the same job. This design removes the red steps from the system-call path. The blue box lists work that runs only on a task switch, an address-space change, a virtual interrupt, or vCPU startup.

The kernelet user task

Stacks and saved user registers in the prototype (a) and this design (b). The blue arrows show a later switch from A to B.

A carrier keeps its own Linux stack, and each task has its own kernelet stack. On entry from user mode, Linux saves the current user task’s registers in pt_regs at the top of the carrier’s Linux stack. The kernelet runs each task’s code on that task’s kernelet stack.

While a user task stays current on the carrier, its registers stay in pt_regs. The handler accesses them through a handle borrowed from vOSTD. For a system call, exception or kernel event without a task switch, vOSTD copies no registers at the handoff. This removes the two copies in item 2.

When another user task needs pt_regs, vOSTD saves the previous task’s registers at the top of that task’s kernelet stack. It then copies the incoming task’s registers from its save area into pt_regs before that task’s code continues. A kernel task has no user registers, so vOSTD saves or installs nothing for it.

The vOSTD interface

In the prototype, the kernel proper calls execute, which stays suspended on the kernelet stack while user code runs. In this design, vOSTD controls user execution and calls the kernel proper’s event callbacks. The handler returns before the task re-enters user mode. vOSTD can change the path between user mode and the handler without changing the handler.

Each user task holds its handler in its data. The kernel proper implements UserEventHandler for its thread type, such as Asterinas’s Thread. Each user task stores an Arc of that type as its data. Callbacks borrow the current task’s handler; for Asterinas, self is &Thread. vOSTD also passes &CurrentTask, the only way to access the task’s local data.

Proposed: vOSTD’s interface for user events.

/// The type of the user tasks' data: each user task's data is an `Arc` of
/// this type. vOSTD calls these methods on the current task's data.
pub trait UserEventHandler: Send + Sync + 'static {
    /// Called once on the new task, before its first entry into user mode.
    fn init(&self, task: &CurrentTask, user: &mut UserMode) {}
    /// Handles one event of the current task; returns when done. It may sleep
    /// or be preempted.
    fn handle(&self, event: UserEvent, task: &CurrentTask, user: &mut UserMode);
    /// Asked before every return to user mode: whether the task ends.
    fn should_exit(&self, task: &CurrentTask, user: &UserMode) -> bool;
    /// Returns true if a virtual interrupt received in user mode left work
    /// for the kernel proper. Otherwise, vOSTD skips `handle`.
    fn has_kernel_event(&self, task: &CurrentTask, user: &UserMode) -> bool { false }
    /// Called with virtual interrupts masked just before user mode, to load
    /// the task's floating-point and segment state.
    fn pre_user_run(&self, task: &CurrentTask, user: &UserMode, guard: &DisabledLocalIrqGuard) {}
}

#[derive(Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub enum UserEvent { Syscall, Exception, KernelEvent }

The kernel proper marks its handler implementation with #[ostd::user_event_handler], which exports six symbols for vOSTD to use.

Rust: TypeId. A TypeId identifies a concrete Rust type. vOSTD stores task data as a Box<dyn Any + Send + Sync> and queries the value’s type ID through Any. It compares this ID with the handler’s exported ID without knowing the handler type. data.as_ref().type_id() identifies the stored value; data.type_id() identifies the box itself. See the Rust documentation on smart pointers and Any.

Generated symbolPurpose
__ostd_user_data_type_idReturn the TypeId of Arc<Thread> for the Thread implementation.
__ostd_user_initCall init once on the new task, before its first entry into user mode.
__ostd_user_handleCall handle for a system call, an exception or a kernel event.
__ostd_user_should_exitCall should_exit before a return to user mode.
__ostd_user_has_kernel_eventCheck whether a kernel event needs handle.
__ostd_user_pre_user_runCall pre_user_run with virtual interrupts masked before user mode.

TaskOptions::new_user(context) takes initial user register values from context; .data(value) supplies the handler state. Before a user task can run, build compares the final data value’s TypeId with the ID returned by __ostd_user_data_type_id. For Thread, the value must be an Arc<Thread>; missing or mismatched data causes build to return Error::InvalidArgs. spawn calls build and propagates this error. build writes the initial registers to the save area at the top of the new task’s kernelet stack. On the first switch, vOSTD installs these registers in the carrier’s pt_regs and calls init on the new task before entering user mode. Objects needed across events remain in task data or local data.

Each callback borrows the current task and a UserMode handle from vOSTD. vOSTD creates the handle and lends it for the callback’s duration. The handle provides access to the task’s registers in the carrier’s pt_regs and its exception state. UserMode is neither Send nor Sync. Through UserContext, the kernel proper can update user registers while the segment fields and privileged flag bits remain fixed.

A callback may sleep while another user task runs on the same carrier and reuses its pt_regs. A &mut reference held across sleep would conflict with accesses by the switch path and the other task. Restoring the values before resumption would not remove this conflict. The callback therefore borrows a UserContext on its own kernelet stack, without borrowing pt_regs. Its methods read and write fields through raw pointers to the frame, without creating references to it. The kernel proper calls the same methods as before.

A switched-out task does not execute its callback or access registers through its handle. The task resumes on the same vCPU, and the handle keeps the same pt_regs address. If another user task took ownership of pt_regs, vOSTD restores the suspended task’s registers from its stack save area before resuming the callback. The callback then accesses its own task’s registers through the same handle.

Porting a kernel proper

The kernel proper replaces its execute loop with callbacks. Event handling moves to handle, the exit check to should_exit, and one-time setup on the new task to init. Objects that the loop created once and reused, such as the process reference, become task data. Each thread holds a strong process reference that callbacks borrow, avoiding reference-count updates on each event. This creates a reference cycle, because the process also holds its tasks. The reaper breaks the cycle by removing the main thread from the process’s task set. References that keep the thread alive can delay process destruction, so cleanup must run earlier. Process exit cancels the timers, and the reaper sends the pidfd’s hang-up event.

Proposed: Asterinas’s user thread from create_new_user_task, written against the handler. The wait while the process is stopped and the panic catching are omitted.

/// Borrows the state that the thread and its task hold.
fn context<'a>(thread: &'a Thread, task: &'a CurrentTask) -> Context<'a> {
    let posix_thread = thread.as_posix_thread().unwrap();
    Context {
        process: posix_thread.process_ref(),                // held by the thread, as in Linux
        thread_local: task.as_thread_local().unwrap(),
        posix_thread,
        thread,
        task,
    }
}

#[ostd::user_event_handler]
impl UserEventHandler for Thread {
    fn init(&self, task: &CurrentTask, _: &mut UserMode) {  // was before the loop
        let ctx = context(self, task);
        let child_tid_ptr = ctx.thread_local.set_child_tid().get();
        if is_userspace_vaddr(child_tid_ptr) {
            let _ = current_userspace!().write_val(child_tid_ptr, &ctx.posix_thread.tid());
        }
        if ctx.posix_thread.tid() == FIRST_POSIX_TID {
            crate::init::on_first_process_startup(&ctx);
        }
    }
    fn handle(&self, event: UserEvent, task: &CurrentTask, user: &mut UserMode) {
        let ctx = context(self, task);
        let exception = user.take_exception();
        let user_ctx = user.context_mut();
        match event {
            UserEvent::Exception => {
                ctx.thread_local.set_orig_syscall_ret(None);
                handle_exception(&ctx, user_ctx, exception.unwrap())
            }
            UserEvent::Syscall => {
                ctx.thread_local.set_orig_syscall_ret(Some(user_ctx.syscall_ret()));
                let res = ctx.posix_thread.ptrace_may_stop_on_syscall(&ctx, user_ctx);
                if !matches!(res, PtraceStopResult::Interrupted) {
                    handle_syscall(&ctx, user_ctx);
                    ctx.posix_thread.ptrace_may_stop_on_syscall(&ctx, user_ctx);
                }
            }
            UserEvent::KernelEvent => {
                ctx.thread_local.set_orig_syscall_ret(None);
            }
        }
        if ctx.thread.is_exited() {
            return;                                         // the loop's check before signals
        }
        handle_pending_signal(user_ctx, &ctx);
    }
    fn should_exit(&self, _: &CurrentTask, _: &UserMode) -> bool {
        self.is_exited()                                    // the loop's exit check
    }
    fn has_kernel_event(&self, task: &CurrentTask, _: &UserMode) -> bool {
        context(self, task).has_pending()                   // was a hook of `execute`
    }
    fn pre_user_run(&self, task: &CurrentTask, _: &UserMode, guard: &DisabledLocalIrqGuard) {
        let thread_local = task.as_thread_local().unwrap();
        thread_local.supp_user_context().before_user_exec(guard);   // was a hook of `execute`
    }
}

TaskOptions::new_user(user_ctx)
    .data(thread)                                           // an `Arc<Thread>`
    .local_data(thread_local)
    .spawn()
    .unwrap();

The handoff protocol

An event enters the kernelet through a Linux hook and leaves through a new Linux entry point. The kernelet never returns to the hook.

In: from the hook to the handler

The syscall hook masks virtual interrupts, switches to the current task’s kernelet stack, and enters vOSTD with the event type. vOSTD reads the current task and event state from the vCPU record. Virtual interrupts stay masked until these reads finish, preventing another task’s events from overwriting the record before vOSTD reads it. vOSTD then unmasks virtual interrupts and calls the kernel proper’s handler. For a kernel event, vOSTD calls the handler only if has_kernel_event returns true. The resume hook uses the same path for exceptions and virtual interrupts.

Out: from the handler to user mode

Proposed, simplified: vOSTD’s entry for a user event.

extern "C" fn user_event_entry(raw_event: u64) -> ! {
    let event = UserEvent::from_raw(raw_event);
    // The hook entered with virtual interrupts masked:
    // no task switch can happen before these reads.
    let task = current_task();
    let data = task.data().as_ref()
        as *const (dyn core::any::Any + Send + Sync) as *const ();
    let mut user = UserMode::for_current_carrier(); // also takes the exception, if any
    unmask_virtual_interrupts();
    // SAFETY: `build` checked this user task's data type. The data cannot
    // be replaced, and the current task keeps it alive during these calls.
    let should_exit = unsafe {
        if event != UserEvent::KernelEvent || __ostd_user_has_kernel_event(data, &task, &user) {
            __ostd_user_handle(data, event, &task, &mut user);
        }
        __ostd_user_should_exit(data, &task, &user)
    };
    if should_exit {
        exit_current_task();
    }
    assert_no_guard_held();                     // a guard kept alive with mem::forget
    preempt_if_wanted();                        // the scheduler's chance before user mode
    let guard = mask_virtual_interrupts();
    // SAFETY: The same task and checked data remain alive after rescheduling.
    unsafe { __ostd_user_pre_user_run(data, &task, &user, &guard) };
    user_run()                                  // never returns
}

After the handler returns, vOSTD completes the checks and callbacks above, then calls user_run. Virtual interrupts remain masked after pre_user_run. The endovisor unmasks them before invoking Linux’s return-to-user path. user_run leaves through kernelet_return_to_user, which starts a call chain on the carrier’s Linux stack below the saved pt_regs. Linux runs its exit work on this chain and returns to user mode.

Task switch

Each vCPU records which user task owns its carrier’s pt_regs. vOSTD copies registers only when switching to a user task other than the owner. It saves the owner’s registers in that task’s save area, installs the incoming task’s registers, and records the new owner. Switches to kernel tasks or back to the owner copy nothing.

vOSTD copies registers in after_switching_to, on the incoming task’s stack with virtual interrupts masked. The copies finish before the task’s own code continues, so the task finds its registers in pt_regs.

  • First run. build allocates the task’s kernelet stack and writes the initial registers to its save area. The first switch installs these registers in the carrier’s pt_regs before init runs. A vCPU without an owner has nothing to save.
  • Owner exit. The exiting owner’s registers are no longer needed. With virtual interrupts masked, exit_current clears the owner record and removes the task from the run queue. The task cannot run again, preventing the owner record from referring to its freed stack.

A task stays on the vCPU where it first runs. Asterinas’s scheduler has no load balancing. Its select_cpu chooses a CPU by load for a new thread and otherwise returns the thread’s last CPU. This design therefore keeps tasks on their vCPUs.

Three parts of this design rely on the rule:

  • Lazy saving. A switched-out task’s registers may still be in its vCPU’s pt_regs.
  • Sleeping callbacks. A callback may keep its UserContext borrow while sleeping. The stored address still identifies the same carrier’s pt_regs.
  • vCPU lookups. vOSTD assumes that a task stays on the same vCPU between finding its record and using it.

Every scheduler used with vOSTD must keep each task on the vCPU where it first runs. Load balancing would require saving a task’s registers from its old carrier’s pt_regs and making vCPU lookups safe across migration.

Invariants

  • Handler data. Each user task keeps an Arc of the registered handler type alive throughout its callbacks. build checks the type; safe APIs cannot replace the data afterwards.
  • Register owner. While a user task runs, in the kernel or in user mode, it owns its vCPU’s pt_regs (Task switch).
  • No reference to pt_regs. Neither the kernel proper nor vOSTD creates a Rust reference to the carrier’s pt_regs (The vOSTD interface).
  • vCPU binding. Every scheduler must keep each task on the vCPU where it first runs.
  • Return state. vOSTD’s register writes keep segment fields and privileged flag bits fixed. The endovisor sets orig_ax to -1 before Linux’s exit work (Implementation and integration).
  • Handler return. The handler returns before its task enters user mode.
  • Masked entry. vOSTD reads the current task and the event state with virtual interrupts masked (In).
  • Services. vOSTD does not switch tasks while a service runs.

Alternatives and tradeoffs

One Linux task per kernelet task

Each kernelet task has a separate Linux task, Linux stack and pt_regs.

  • Gain: No register copy at a task switch, and no shared Linux stack.
  • Cost: A kernelet task switch becomes a Linux sleep and wakeup. Linux’s scheduler decides when the woken task runs. Each sandbox needs one Linux task per tenant thread.

Per-task Linux stacks

Each user task has its own Linux stack, where Linux saves user registers in pt_regs and UserContext accesses them in place. On a switch to a user task, the endovisor installs that task’s Linux stack as the carrier’s by replacing task_struct.stack and the stack top.

  • Gain: A system call copies no registers and takes 63.5 ns, 5.8 ns more than native getpid (Validation and evidence). Task switches also copy no registers, and tasks may move between vCPUs.
  • Cost: It breaks Linux’s stack-lifetime invariant, requires invasive changes, and uses more memory. Linux requires a fixed kernel stack throughout each task’s life to locate pt_regs, check stack bounds and walk the stack. Each Linux stack takes 16 KiB.

Switcher at IA32_LSTAR

PVM (Huang et al., SOSP 2023) runs secure-container guests in hardware ring 3 inside a leased cloud VM. It points IA32_LSTAR at a short assembly switcher in the host kernel. A guest syscall then reaches the guest kernel without the hypervisor’s exit handler. PVM calls this a direct switch.

x86: IA32_LSTAR. This model-specific register stores the entry address for 64-bit system calls. When user code executes syscall, the processor enters ring 0 at this address. The kernel sets the register with the privileged wrmsr instruction, normally once at boot.

For kernelets, IA32_LSTAR could point to a switcher while a carrier runs, letting its syscall skip Linux’s system-call entry.

One system call under PVM’s direct switch and under this design. Numbers mark each change between ring 3 and ring 0.

  • Gain: Linux’s system-call entry code stays unchanged, and ordinary tasks continue to use it.
  • Cost: The switcher must redo the required work of Linux’s entry and exit code. It skips seccomp, ptrace, audit and syscall tracing for carrier calls. Each switch to or from a carrier writes IA32_LSTAR. If Linux runs in a VM whose hypervisor traps this write, it causes a VM exit.

Switcher in Linux’s entry code

After entry_SYSCALL_64 saves the registers, new assembly code checks the current task’s syscall work bits before Linux’s C entry code runs. If only the kernelet’s bit is set, the code jumps to the switcher. Otherwise Linux takes its C path, where enabled checks such as seccomp and ptrace run before the kernelet hook.

  • Gain: A carrier with only the kernelet’s work bit set skips Linux’s C entry code. Switches to and from carriers do not write IA32_LSTAR, avoiding the VM exits caused by that write.
  • Cost: Every task runs two extra loads, a comparison and a branch on each system call. The switcher must redo the required work of Linux’s C entry and exit code.

One system call, step by step. Positions 1 and 2 are the two switchers above; position 3 is this design’s hook. Each bar shows the Linux steps that a position skips on the way in.

This design keeps the hook in syscall_trace_enter, after user dispatch, ptrace and seccomp. Linux runs its enabled checks before the hook, its entry assembly stays unchanged, and no switch writes IA32_LSTAR. Keeping Linux’s C entry code and exit work means this position skips the least Linux code of the three. Both switchers must reproduce required work, such as enabling interrupts and checking pending work, across Linux versions and configurations. An earlier trace without the mirror measured about 20 ns across Linux’s entry, hook call and exit work. The switcher in Linux’s entry code would retain part of this work; its net saving is not measured.

Implementation and integration

Linux

  • Add kernelet_return_to_user. It completes the exit work from the carrier’s last entry, then uses the same checks as do_syscall_64 to choose SYSRET or IRET. After a task switch, this work can belong to another task’s system call. The checks select IRET if the installed registers do not permit SYSRET.

Endovisor

  • Event entry. The hook masks virtual interrupts, switches to the current task’s kernelet stack below its save area, and never returns. This replaces klet_switch. On syscall entry, the hook sets regs->ax = regs->orig_ax so the kernel proper can read the syscall number through UserContext::rax().
  • Services. A service runs on the carrier’s Linux stack and returns to its call site on the kernelet stack. The endovisor delivers no upcall during a service, so vOSTD does not switch tasks.
  • Address of pt_regs. The endovisor publishes the address of the carrier’s pt_regs in the vCPU record.
  • Reset of orig_ax. Before each return to user mode, the endovisor sets orig_ax to -1. Otherwise, a value of rax from rt_sigreturn or another task could look like a restart code.

Linux: orig_ax and system-call restart. Linux stores the syscall number in the orig_ax field of pt_regs, or -1 for an interrupt or exception. Linux may restart a call if a signal is pending, orig_ax differs from -1, and rax holds a restart code such as -ERESTARTSYS. Before returning to user mode, it puts the call number back in rax and moves rip back over the syscall instruction.

vOSTD

  • Handler entry points. The macro emits the six functions in the kernel proper with #[unsafe(no_mangle)] and the Rust ABI. The five callback entries take the task’s data address as *const (), then the trait arguments except self. For Thread, each borrows &Arc<Thread> and calls the method with thread.as_ref(), without cloning the Arc or creating &mut Thread. Each image must define one registration.
  • UserContext with two forms. Owned holds independent register values from UserContext::default() for initialization or clone() for a snapshot. build stores the initial values as RawUserRegs in the task’s stack save area. Frame is the handle that context_mut lends to a callback. On first run or event entry, UserMode gets the carrier’s pt_regs address from the vCPU record. Task switching copies registers between the save area and pt_regs; the handle’s form and address stay unchanged. Frame methods read and write through raw pointers and return values, without creating references to pt_regs. general_regs and general_regs_mut return objects containing copies; references into these objects point to the copies. The mutable object borrows its parent UserContext until it writes the copy back on drop.
  • No Send or Sync. UserContext implements neither trait. Asterinas creates and uses each context within one task, so it does not need Send. Task data, local_data and entry closures require Send, which prevents them from storing or transferring a UserContext.
  • Replaced context. If init or handle replaces the borrowed UserContext with an owned one, vOSTD installs those registers in the current task’s pt_regs after the callback returns. The same field restrictions apply as for other context writes.
  • Save area and owner. Each user task reserves a pt_regs-sized save area at the top of its kernelet stack. The first entry and every event entry start below it. Each vCPU records its pt_regs owner. after_switching_to copies registers when the owner changes; with virtual interrupts masked, exit_current clears an exiting owner’s record and removes the task from the run queue.
  • Default scheduler. vOSTD’s FIFO scheduler records the vCPU where a task first runs and wakes the task there.
  • Fixed fields. vOSTD keeps segment fields and privileged flag bits fixed on every user-register write, in both UserContext forms and when installing a task’s registers. This includes general_regs_mut writeback and replacement with an owned context. set_rflags keeps only RFLAGS_USER bits: those Linux lets a debugger set, plus ID. It always sets IF and bit 1.

x86: user-mode return state. CS selects the code segment, SS selects the stack segment, and RFLAGS contains status and control bits. This design fixes the following values for return to 64-bit user mode:

FieldFixed valuePurpose
CS0x33Select the 64-bit user code segment.
SS0x2bSelect the user stack segment; RSP supplies the stack address.
RFLAGS.IF1Allow maskable hardware interrupts in user mode.
RFLAGS.IOPL0Prevent the kernel proper from raising user I/O privileges.

The segment values and flag definitions come from Linux v6.12’s segment header and flags header.

Proposed: UserContext::set_rflags for owned registers and the carrier frame.

/// Sets rflags: only the bits a user program may hold are taken, and the
/// interrupt flag and bit 1 are always set.
#[inline(always)]
pub fn set_rflags(&mut self, rflags: usize) {
    let value = (rflags & RFLAGS_USER) | RFLAGS_FIXED;
    match &mut self.storage {
        ContextStorage::Owned(regs) => regs.rflags = value,
        // SAFETY: The current task owns this live carrier frame.
        // This write does not create a reference to the frame.
        ContextStorage::Frame(frame) => unsafe {
            core::ptr::addr_of_mut!((**frame).rflags).write(value)
        },
    }
}

For example, set_rflags(0) stores 0x202, with IF and bit 1 set. set_rflags(3 << 12) also stores 0x202: the requested IOPL = 3 is cleared.

Update the Accepted Design chapters listed under Affected chapters together with the corresponding prototype changes.

Validation and evidence

We measured this design and per-task Linux stacks in one run, with the same Linux configuration. Both share the handler and the return path. A QEMU/KVM guest runs patched Linux 6.12 with two vCPUs pinned to separate host cores. The kernelet uses one vCPU and one user task, and its handler returns a constant. Each configuration boots five times in shuffled order.

The time of one system call

The table reports the mean of the five per-boot averages; range gives the smallest and largest. Each ratio uses the native time on that design’s Linux build. The prototype’s row comes from another run.

SettingTime (ns)Range (ns)Ratio to native
Native getpid57.557.3–57.81.00
The prototype125.1123.9–125.92.22
Per-task Linux stacks63.563.1–63.81.10
This design63.762.7–65.61.11

This design and per-task Linux stacks take the same time; their ranges overlap. The times include Linux’s own entry and exit work, which this proposal does not change.

Functional checks

On the final implementation of this design, system calls and task scheduling work correctly.

  • System calls. Handlers received the expected arguments; user tasks received the expected results and retained their other general registers, including after a handler slept.
  • Scheduling. Several user tasks shared a vCPU, blocked, woke and switched through kernel tasks without losing their saved registers. Each resumed on its original vCPU.

Open questions

  • Floating-point and segment-base state across task switches. pt_regs holds only general registers. Linux keeps FPU registers and FS and GS bases in the CPU. It saves them only on carrier switches or, for the FPU, when kernel code uses it. During kernelet execution, a task’s FPU state can therefore be in the CPU or the carrier’s thread.fpu. Task switches must coordinate with Linux to save and load this state. Linux marks saved FPU state with TIF_NEED_FPU_LOAD and reloads it before user mode.

Decision and follow-up

Pending review.

Appendix: the prototype’s mechanisms

A tenant system call uses these prototype mechanisms.

  • Kernelet. The tenant’s kernel, linked into one image: the kernel proper, written in safe Rust, and vOSTD, which provides OSTD’s interface on Linux.

  • Carrier. A Linux task that runs one virtual CPU of the kernelet. The tenant program runs on it in user mode (Carriers).

  • Endovisor. The Linux module that serves kernelets. The syscall hook and every klet_* function in the figure belong to it.

  • Syscall hook. Our Linux patch adds this hook after seccomp in the system-call entry. Linux calls it for each carrier syscall. The hook passes the call to the kernelet, and Linux skips its own system-call table (Carrier hooks and patch overview).

  • execute. OSTD’s interface for running user code, called in a loop by the kernel proper. execute enters user mode and returns on a system call, exception, or event for the kernel proper. The kernel proper handles the event and calls execute again (Into user mode and back). The hundred-line kernel uses this loop:

    Simplified from prototype/kernels/hello/src/lib.rs: create_user_task.

    let mut user_mode = UserMode::new(create_user_context());
loop {
    let return_reason = user_mode.execute(&DummyUserHooks);
    let user_context = user_mode.context_mut();
    if ReturnReason::UserSyscall == return_reason {
        handle_syscall(user_context, &vm_space);
    }
}

  • Two stacks. A task’s Linux kernel stack is empty on return to user mode. execute returns on a system call, exception or kernel event; its frames remain on the task’s kernelet stack while the program runs. klet_switch switches between the hook’s kernel-mode context on the carrier’s Linux stack and the current task’s context on its kernelet stack. The hook waits while the kernelet runs; the task waits inside execute while the user program runs (Two stacks).

  • UserContext. The kernelet’s copy of a task’s user registers. On kernel entry, Linux saves them in pt_regs at the top of the carrier’s Linux stack. The hook copies them into the task’s UserContext and back before returning to user mode (Into user mode and back).

  • Service table. The kernelet image imports no host symbols. It calls the endovisor only through a function-pointer table received at startup. The user_run entry enters user mode (The kernelet ABI).

  • Virtual CPU record. One shared record per virtual CPU, holding pending virtual interrupts and the address space last activated with vm_activate. Each tenant process has its own address space, a Linux mm. The carrier switches to the activated space before returning to user mode (Tasks and virtual CPUs, Tenant memory on Linux’s page tables).

  • Virtual interrupts. A kernelet timer tick or request from another virtual CPU. The endovisor sets a pending bit, then checks whether the carrier is in or entering user mode and notifies Linux if so. Before checking pending events, user_run publishes KLET_USER. Memory barriers order both sequences, so an interrupt raised during this handoff is either seen by user_run or causes a Linux notification. Linux clears the notification flag before calling the resume hook, preserving notifications raised during the hook (Virtual interrupts).

  • The mirror and watch timer. The prototype uses these mechanisms to coordinate with Linux’s scheduler; the timer also delivers virtual timer ticks. Their implementation is described in Sharing a processor politely.

2026-11-04 Coordinate the two schedulers

  • Status: Draft
  • Author(s): Plucky923
  • Discussion: Not yet available.
  • Affected chapters: Tasks and virtual CPUs, Sharing a processor politely, Virtual interrupts, and What it costs.
  • Implementation or experiments: Not yet implemented.

Summary

Linux schedules the carriers that provide a sandbox’s virtual CPUs, while the kernelet schedules its own tasks on those virtual CPUs. This proposal coordinates their decisions through direct access to Linux’s scheduling state, retaining the existing mirror and watch timer. The additional host load and location accessors and the task-placement policy remain to be specified and implemented. Their performance benefits remain unmeasured.

Problem and motivation

In the accepted design, each virtual CPU has one carrier for the sandbox’s lifetime. Linux schedules carriers on physical CPUs. The kernelet schedules tasks on virtual CPUs. Without coordination, each scheduler may lack the other’s runtime information.

1. Guest scheduling without host runtime information

The kernelet scheduler may make poor task-placement decisions without current information about host CPU load and its carriers’ physical cache locality.

Virtualization: runtime hypervisor-internal states. CPS (§2.2, ASPLOS ’23) reports that guest kernels can make poor task-placement decisions without current information about Runtime Hypervisor-internal States (RHS). The hypervisor maintains these states, including physical CPU load and the current mapping of virtual CPUs to physical cache groups. In the manycore system studied by CPS, a cache group (CG) contains physical cores that share a partition of the last-level cache.

CPS identifies two RHS problems:

  1. Invisible physical CPU load. In CPS’s example, KVM records each virtual CPU’s preemption status in shared memory. A running virtual CPU can read the status of another virtual CPU that the host has preempted. This status does not reveal physical CPU load. In the low-load case, the guest avoids all preempted virtual CPUs when placing new or awakened tasks. This excludes virtual CPUs that could soon resume on physical CPUs with low load, leaving available capacity underused.
  2. Invisible dynamic cache-group mapping. A guest may place communicating threads on virtual CPUs mapped to the same physical cache group. The host can then move one virtual CPU to a physical CPU in another group. Without an updated mapping, the guest cannot account for this loss of locality when placing tasks. In CPS’s experiment, four threads incremented one shared atomic variable. Among the placements tested, throughput was highest when all four threads ran in one cache group (§2.2, Table 1).

2. Host preemption without guest execution information

The kernelet selects the task that runs on each virtual CPU. Linux decides when that virtual CPU’s carrier runs on a physical CPU. Without coordination, Linux does not know which kernelet tasks hold locks or which tasks depend on those lock holders. When ordinary kernel preemption is permitted, Linux may deschedule a carrier while its current kernelet task is in a critical section.

Suppose task A on virtual CPU 0 holds a spin lock that task B on virtual CPU 1 needs. Linux may deschedule A’s carrier while B’s carrier continues to run. A then cannot release the lock, while B may keep spinning and consuming processor time. A remains the current task on its virtual CPU, but cannot resume until Linux schedules its carrier again. This is the lock-holder preemption problem studied in VMs (eCS, §2.1, USENIX ATC ’18).

Virtualization: scheduling interfaces. CPS (§4.1) publishes physical CPU load and cache-group mappings in shared memory to guide guest task placement. eCS (§3) lets the guest mark critical sections in shared memory so the hypervisor can delay virtual CPU preemption. Both avoid a hypercall per access, but must maintain shared state and extend the interface to expose additional host information.

Proposed change

Linux schedules carriers, and the kernelet schedules tasks on their virtual CPUs. The proposal coordinates these decisions through direct access to Linux’s per-CPU state.

Shared access through GS

Linux and x86-64: per-CPU state and GS. Linux keeps a separate area of per-CPU data for each physical CPU. During kernel-mode execution, each CPU’s GS base identifies its own area, allowing code to access the current CPU’s local state.

Linux passes kernel events to vOSTD within the carrier’s host kernel context. The design keeps the host kernel mappings and the current physical CPU’s GS base across Linux, endovisor, and kernelet kernel execution. This lets vOSTD read and update selected host per-CPU fields without a host service call. Each access must follow Linux’s rules for that field, including synchronization.

Task placement and carrier preemption

  1. Host state guides kernelet scheduling. Extend vOSTD to expose host CPU load and carrier cache-group mappings to the kernelet scheduler. For each runnable task, the scheduler can compare candidate virtual CPUs by host contention and cache locality with communicating tasks. GS selects the current physical CPU’s per-CPU area. vOSTD must associate each observation with its carrier and host CPU and account for migration.
  2. Kernelet state guides host preemption. Retain the existing mirror. On entry to the outermost virtual critical section, vOSTD increments Linux’s per-CPU preemption count through GS. Linux’s existing checks then defer ordinary carrier preemption while kernelet code executes inside the section. On exit, vOSTD removes the increment, checks for a host rescheduling request, and yields if needed.

The watch timer remains a fallback when the kernelet delays responding to a host rescheduling request. During a forced yield from a virtual critical section, the endovisor temporarily removes the mirrored increment so that Linux can schedule. It restores the increment before resuming kernelet execution, with the virtual critical section still active. Host-service calls retain their existing blocking and yielding behavior. Coordination of sleeping and waking remains deferred.

Advantages

A conventional x86-64 VM uses its own kernel mappings and GS base. Its kernel therefore needs an explicit interface to access host scheduling state. CPS (§4.1) provides a shared Refer-Table that the guest can read without additional hypercalls after initialization. Direct access to Linux’s original state offers three advantages:

  1. New scheduling inputs without shared copies. When a new scheduling problem needs host information not yet exposed, CPS (§4.1) must extend its Refer-Table and maintain the added state. Our design resolves each CPU’s per-CPU area offset and the required field offsets once during Linux startup. vOSTD then reads live values directly from host per-CPU fields with known layouts, following Linux’s access rules. GS selects the current CPU’s area, while recorded CPU offsets locate other CPUs’ areas. Adding a scheduling input therefore requires no new shared copy or code to keep that copy current.

    Hardware: NUMA nodes. A non-uniform memory access (NUMA) system groups CPUs and memory into nodes. Memory access costs depend on the CPU and memory nodes.

    Examples of host fields that can guide kernelet scheduling include:

    • NUMA node. Linux’s per-CPU numa_node identifies the CPU’s NUMA node. The kernelet can use it to consider memory locality when placing tasks.
    • Shared cache. Linux’s per-CPU sd_llc_id identifies the CPU’s last-level-cache domain. Comparing these IDs can guide the placement of communicating tasks on CPUs that share a cache.
    • CPU load and capacity. Each CPU’s runqueue contains nr_running and cpu_capacity. These fields expose the runnable-task count and Linux’s CPU capacity estimate for task-placement decisions.

  2. Use of existing host controls. The mirror changes the count Linux already checks before preemption. This lets Linux defer ordinary preemption without interpreting a separate guest critical-section flag.

  3. Potentially lower overhead.

    • State publication. CPS (§4.1) publishes each physical CPU’s load degree in a shared page. When the kernelet uses existing host per-CPU fields, vOSTD can read them without maintaining a separate shared representation.
    • Migration updates. When a vCPU migrates, CPS (§5.1) changes the guest mapping to refer to the new physical CPU’s load page. After a carrier migrates, a GS-relative read uses the new CPU’s host state without this extra mapping update.
    • Expected read savings. In CPS (§5.1), the guest reads a load value through a pointer stored in the shared table. For an existing scalar on the current CPU, a fixed-offset GS read can access the host field directly. If a query must reload the shared pointer, GS can reduce the pointer and value reads from two dependent loads to one. We expect lower query latency, especially when the table entry misses in cache and delays the value read. Keeping the shared address in a register or fetching the GS offset from memory can remove this advantage.

2026-11-08 TypedIO

  • Status: Under review
  • Author(s): Codex, with independent Linux, Asterinas, systems-research, and editorial review
  • Discussion: See the pull request for this proposal.
  • Affected chapters: The kernelet ABI, Tasks and virtual CPUs, Stopping a kernelet, and What vOSTD adds on top
  • Implementation or experiments: TypedIO is not implemented; source analysis, design review, and lessons from the Kernelet Vsock experiment

TypedIO is a proposed standard interface for virtual devices in kernelets, serving the role that VirtIO plays in virtual machines. Kernelets can also use traditional VirtIO devices, but TypedIO aims to deliver better performance by exploiting the kernelet architecture.

Motivation and Problem

Kernelets need devices to run useful workloads: block storage for filesystems and vsocks for communication with host applications or other kernelets. Linux already provides the underlying devices and services. The missing piece is a common interface between a kernelet driver, the frontend, and a Linux-side service, the backend, covering discovery, configuration, data transfer, and completion.

Why VirtIO is a poor fit

VirtIO is a natural candidate. It standardizes virtual devices across operating systems, and Asterinas already has VirtIO drivers and queue support. Reusing them could reduce frontend work and preserve compatibility with existing backends.

VirtIO: queues and backend placement. VirtIO defines binary device interfaces independently of the implementation language. Its shared queues, called virtqueues, contain descriptors specifying buffer addresses, lengths, and access directions. Feature negotiation selects optional behavior supported by both sides. See the VirtIO 1.2 specification, especially §§1.4, 2, 3.3, and 5.

Backends process the requests. QEMU can consume virtqueues and issue host I/O in userspace. Linux also provides kernel backends for particular devices, including vhost-net, vhost-vsock, and vhost-scsi; these are not replacements for every QEMU device backend. See the Linux 6.12 vhost drivers.

With kernel vhost, QEMU handles device and queue setup while a Linux backend processes ordinary I/O. This separates setup—the control plane—from routine processing—the data plane. Scheduling and notification costs remain. The vhost interface exposes that setup to userspace.

vhost-user instead places the queue-processing backend in a separate userspace process. QEMU shares queue memory and notification handles with that process; ordinary I/O can bypass QEMU while still requiring userspace backend execution. See the QEMU vhost-user documentation.

Kernelets offer a different starting point from a general-purpose VM: safe Rust drivers, a trusted OS development framework, and an existing service interface to Linux. For this setting, adopting VirtIO as the native interface has two drawbacks:

  • VirtIO loses Rust’s ownership and type contracts at the boundary. A VirtIO block request is a binary operation description plus buffer descriptors; a descriptor’s address and write flag do not express ownership of a Rust buffer. Safe Rust wrappers can enforce such contracts, but they must translate them into the separately specified protocol and reconstruct them at the backend.
  • VirtIO limits optimizations enabled by the kernelet architecture. Kernelets execute alongside the host and one another in a shared kernel address space, with Rust’s type and ownership rules constraining what safe code can access. This combination creates opportunities to reduce copying and coordination overhead. Virtual device interfaces should exploit these opportunities, but VirtIO’s existing interfaces, designed for communication across VM boundaries, limit how fully they can do so.

Design Goals and Requirements

Device generality. Support a broad range of virtual devices, including block, network, vsock, and filesystem devices, without being limited to VirtIO’s device types. Provide common mechanisms for discovery, configuration, data transfer, and notification that simplify both frontend and backend development. Each device protocol defines its operations, message formats, and execution/completion rules in Rust.

Memory safety. Support frontend and backend drivers written entirely in safe Rust, with only small additions to the trusted computing base (TCB), the code relied on for memory safety: minimal vOSTD extensions and a small host framework with audited Linux wrappers. This trusted code must retain and safely reclaim cross-boundary resources despite buggy, stalled, or terminated kernelets, without relying on their destructors or cooperation. Bound and account for retained resources, and reclaim them only after all accesses have ended.

High performance. Exploit the shared kernel address space and Rust’s safety guarantees to reduce data movement and coordination costs. Process backend I/O in the host kernel, and eliminate stream forwarding copies by sharing a kernel buffer directly between endpoints. Explore direct host page-cache access without adding a VM’s second address-translation layer, and avoid duplicate per-page metadata where host records can be reused. VMs also support shared buffers, and virtiofs direct access (DAX) exposes host-cached file data; evaluate TypedIO against these mechanisms and in-kernel vhost backends as well as QEMU.

Asterinas integration. Prefer existing OSTD memory and I/O interfaces, including IrqLine for notifications and DmaStream for making kernelet buffers accessible to the host. Existing Asterinas network, socket, and block I/O stacks should support TypedIO frontends alongside VirtIO frontends, with minimal changes to their callers. Breaking API changes are a last resort.

TypedIO design

Overview

A frontend submits Rust values describing an operation; its backend performs the operation through Linux. vOSTD and the host framework validate access, retain buffers, and deliver results, leaving device behavior to the drivers.

Consider a frontend reading 4 KiB from a disk into an existing OSTD buffer. Both sides use the same BlockProtocol definition, which groups the device’s configuration, request, and reply types through the Protocol trait. Those types implement Message, the contract that permits their values to cross the kernelet–host boundary. A read is expressed as a BlockRequest::Read value containing a sector and destination buffers.

The frontend first finds an authorized disk with discover::<BlockProtocol>(). It examines each Device through config() and opens a bounded request Queue on the selected device.

OSTD: DMA buffers. Direct memory access (DMA) lets a device access memory without a CPU payload copy. OSTD’s DmaCoherent and DmaStream own storage prepared for device access: coherent buffers need no explicit cache synchronization, while streaming buffers provide synchronization methods. Both expose byte access through VmReader and VmWriter. Asterinas’s Slice<Arc<DmaStream>> describes a bounded range while sharing ownership of the DMA object.

For the block read, the frontend supplies a destination range as a Slice<Arc<DmaStream>>. Submitting this value lets the framework retain the buffer independently and give the host write access for the read operation.

A queue supports multiple outstanding requests. Each carries a request tag identifying frontend state; the driver matches arriving completions to their callers. The example uses the frontend’s synchronous wait_reply helper.

Proposed frontend example. The caller supplies queue options, a DMA buffer of at least 4 KiB, and a valid sector. BlockFrontend::new installs the interrupt/completion worker; submit records local state before submission, and wait_reply waits on that state.

let devices = discover::<BlockProtocol>()?;
let device = select_device(&devices)?;
let queue = device.open(options)?;
let frontend = BlockFrontend::new(queue)?;

let destination = Slice::new(Arc::clone(&buffer), 0..4096);
let pending = frontend.submit(BlockRequest::Read {
    sector,
    buffers: [destination].try_into()?, // A bounded list of existing DMA buffer ranges.
})?;
let reply = wait_reply(pending)?;

The host accepts the request into a HostRequest, which retains the destination independently of frontend handles. The registered backend passes it to a Linux adapter. The adapter reports completion only after every host access ends, making the final result available to the frontend.

Completion triggers a virtual interrupt, a software interrupt delivered to a kernelet virtual CPU (vCPU). vOSTD invokes the driver’s IrqLine callback; the driver drains tagged results and chooses which callers to notify. After a successful read, it calls buffer.sync_from_device(0..4096) before reading the bytes. Trusted records retain storage until host access ends even if a caller abandons the request or the kernelet dies.

Streams use byte pipes, shared storage carrying ordered bytes. A connection handshake delivers Producer and Consumer endpoints; each reserves a range, transfers bytes, and commits progress. Pipes reuse the same discovery, typed messages, resource checks, and interrupts as block I/O.

Architecture

The block and stream paths separate driver policy from trusted transfer mechanisms:

Layer organization. Established stream payload travels directly through pipe endpoints.

%%{init: {"htmlLabels": false, "flowchart": {"htmlLabels": false}}}%%
flowchart TB
    A["Application I/O"] --> K["Safe Asterinas<br/>frontend drivers"]
    K --> P["Device protocols:<br/>Rust requests and replies"]
    P --> V["vOSTD: queues, DMA buffers,<br/>byte pipes, IRQ delivery"]
    V --> H["Trusted host framework:<br/>ownership and lifecycle records"]
    H --> B["Safe Linux<br/>device backends"]
    B --> L["Trusted Linux wrappers<br/>and existing drivers"]

The trusted components are vOSTD, the host framework and wrappers, the message-derivation machinery, the loader, and the underlying Linux kernel. Device policies remain in safe frontend and backend code. Safe Rust does not itself prove those policies correct or ensure that a device makes progress.

LayerResponsibilityExcluded responsibility
vOSTDValidate DMA ranges, retain local storage, expose bounded access, dispatch IRQ callbacksPorts, connection policy, block ordering policy
Kernel properSocket state, block frontend, completion dispatch and task wakeupsDirect host-memory access or Linux calls
Host frameworkValidate resource access, retain storage, account for requests, handle teardownDevice-specific interpretation of data
Safe backendAuthorization and device protocol, Linux adapter selectionUnchecked pointer access, device-access lifetimes

Protocol and message types

The frontend and backend need to agree on operations without sharing heap ownership or assuming that objects owned by a kernelet instance remain valid after that instance terminates. Protocol groups a device’s configuration, request, and reply types; each must implement Message so the framework can transfer it safely. Both sides know every shared protocol and generated transfer-view definition; private fields restrict construction and access, not knowledge of the type.

A type is exchangeable in TypedIO when the framework can transfer its value between a kernelet instance and the host, or between instances, without giving safe receiver code access to unretained memory or depending on the sending instance to run cleanup. The proposed unsafe trait Message expresses this contract. A trusted derive macro checks ordinary structs and enums recursively; vOSTD supplies audited implementations for primitives, supported containers, and resource handles. Application and device code use the trait through safe APIs. Each resource’s audited hooks specify its permitted transfer directions.

Data representation: schemas. A schema specifies a message’s variants, fields, types, and size limits. A schema digest is a hash identifying a schema; equality detects agreement, not whether a particular message is valid or authorized.

Proposed type contracts; generated transfer code and routine definitions are omitted.

/// Marks a type whose generated or audited transfer satisfies the exchange contract.
///
/// # Safety
/// Implementations must satisfy the documented exchangeability requirements.
/// The schema must describe the transfer representation and resource hooks exactly.
pub unsafe trait Message: Sized + 'static {
    const SCHEMA: SchemaDigest;
}

/// Groups a device's configuration, request, and reply types.
pub trait Protocol {
    type Config: Message;
    type Request: Message;
    type Reply: Message;
    const ID: ProtocolId;
}

Exchangeable message types

Rust: copying and representation. Copy permits duplicating a value within Rust’s ownership rules; shared references and raw pointers can also be Copy. An application binary interface (ABI) specifies how compiled components represent and pass data. #[repr(C)] specifies a layout convention, but permits padding and does not change the representation of nested fields. Neither property keeps referenced storage alive after its owner releases it. See the Copy contract and layout rules.

An acceptable message must satisfy all of these requirements:

PropertyRequirement
Recursive admissibilityEvery field belongs to the supported set below, including fields of inactive enum variants and generic type arguments used as fields. No blanket implementation for Copy types is allowed.
Bounded transferThe schema bounds metadata size and container lengths. Transfer cannot follow arbitrary object graphs or depend on unbounded allocation.
Independent lifetimeThe receiver owns its local value. Any usable external resource has framework-managed retention; no sender-local reference, allocator, callback, or destructor is needed to use or retire it.
Stable, valid captureSubmission captures a valid typed value while its storage is live and protected from mutation. Ordinary message fields have no interior mutability; concurrent access to DMA payload is governed separately.
Compatible representationGenerated transfer code preserves field values and type invariants, handles padding safely, and agrees on layout wherever it copies a representation directly. A schema digest alone does not prove these properties.

The framework initially supports these types and compositions:

TypeAdmission rule
Scalars(), integers, floats, bool, and char; target-dependent widths such as usize are part of the compatibility check.
Structs, newtypes, enums, tuples, and arraysEvery field or element is exchangeable. User-defined types derive Message and have no custom Drop; a type need not implement Copy.
Option<T> and Result<T, E>Their contained types are exchangeable. The generated transfer preserves the active variant.
Framework BoundedVec<T>Elements are exchangeable and the schema fixes a maximum length. Only live elements transfer; a local allocation pointer never does. Ordinary Vec, String, Box, and arbitrary Arc<T> have no implementation; supported DMA owners are the explicit exception below.
Thin raw pointers, *const T and *mut T for sized TTransfer only the opaque address. The pointee is neither traversed nor retained and need not be exchangeable. No host access authority follows from the address.
DMA resourcesDmaCoherent, DmaStream<D>, their Arc forms, and bounded Slice views permit request-scoped transfer from a kernelet to host-local access handles. They do not import DMA owners into another kernelet or into replies. Neither the DMA object’s representation nor its Arc pointer crosses the boundary.
Pipe endpoints: Producer, ConsumerAudited hooks move the producer or consumer role and retain its pipe storage. These types are neither Copy nor Clone; transfer is rejected while a reservation is outstanding. ProducerReservation<'_> and ConsumerReservation<'_> are local borrows and are not exchangeable.

References, function pointers, trait objects, unions, MaybeUninit, and interior-mutable types have no implementation in this version. References and safe function pointers would let safe receiver code access data or execute code whose lifetime has not been established. Other unsupported types require a separate audited extension; wrapping them in a Copy or #[repr(C)] struct does not make them admissible.

Raw pointers are permitted as data because safe code cannot dereference them. Transfer preserves their addresses without promising that they remain valid for memory access, even if echoed back to the sender. Trusted host adapters obtain buffer access through retained DMA resources, never by trusting a raw-pointer field. This distinction follows Rust’s separation between carrying a pointer and using it to access memory.

Proposed admission examples; Message is the trusted derive macro.

#[repr(C)]
#[derive(Clone, Copy, Message)]
struct ReadRange { sector: u64, bytes: u64 } // Exchangeable; direct-copy candidate.

#[derive(Message)]
struct ReadRequest {
    range: ReadRange,
    destination: Slice<Arc<DmaStream>>, // Exchangeable through framework retention.
}

#[repr(C)]
#[derive(Clone, Copy)]
struct Borrowed<'a> { bytes: &'a [u8] } // Cannot derive Message.

Proposed block protocol declaration. BlockConfig and BLOCK_PROTOCOL_ID belong to the shared definition; request and reply types appear in the block device design.

pub struct BlockProtocol;

impl Protocol for BlockProtocol {
    type Config = BlockConfig;
    type Request = BlockRequest;
    type Reply = BlockReply;
    const ID: ProtocolId = BLOCK_PROTOCOL_ID;
}

Transfer representation

Shared Rust types define message structure; generated transfer code handles representation differences between independently compiled components. The trusted macro derives Message, a schema descriptor, and transfer code from its types. Generated field bounds and compile-time assertions check nested types and concrete generic instantiations; the macro does not guess their properties from names. The macro and its support code belong to the TCB.

Exchangeability does not require field-by-field serialization on every call. A Copy value with a verified layout, no uninitialized padding, and no fields requiring resource-transfer hooks can use a direct metadata copy, including opaque raw-pointer fields with compatible representations. For example, ReadRange can use this path after layout checks. #[repr(C)] alone does not establish these conditions. Default-layout structs, tuples, and enums remain admissible through generated field transfer, without assuming their native representations match across builds.

Resource-bearing messages need additional work even when their scalar fields can be copied directly. A block read transfers its sector and buffer descriptors while trusted hooks retain the backing and create host-local access handles. Bounded lists transfer their elements into reserved receiver storage. These operations still cost CPU time; the design eliminates unnecessary representation conversion, not ownership accounting or metadata capture.

The kernelet–host boundary uses a small versioned service ABI carrying scalars and bounded transfer records. Metadata, the operation description and resource identifiers, is captured into host-owned storage before asynchronous dispatch; payload, the actual I/O bytes, stays in retained storage. Records contain initialized fields with padding omitted or initialized, and no sender-local owning objects. Raw-pointer fields travel as addresses; worker queues carry these records rather than granting arbitrary message types Send.

Build and loader checks must admit only trusted generated or audited implementations, including their transitive dependencies and macro expansions; a top-level unsafe lint alone does not establish the transfer invariants. Protocol definitions remain above vOSTD, so adding an operation requires no handwritten device-specific unsafe code.

Submission captures a valid typed value while it remains stable, preserving bool, char, and enum validity. Byte-oriented ingress must instead validate before constructing Rust values. Runtime checks still enforce sizes, device state, rights, and retention. Capture is synchronous and bounded; the host retains no caller argument address afterward.

Device discovery and configuration

A frontend must find a device it is allowed to use and learn its limits before submitting I/O. discover<P> returns matching Device<P> handles; config exposes an attachment’s configuration, and open creates its request queue.

Protection: capabilities. A capability is an unforgeable handle granting specified access to a particular resource, such as permission to read a file. Possession confers that access; a resource identifier alone does not. Systems that reuse handle slots can attach an object generation to distinguish a removed resource from its replacement and reject stale handles.

The holder, the host process that creates and controls a kernelet, attaches devices before start, assigning a device instance and restricting its resources, peers, and access rights. Discovery returns a snapshot of those attachments. Live reconfiguration uses removal followed by a new generation; removal rejects new submissions, signals the driver through its IRQ line, and lets existing accesses finish. Handles from an old generation cannot name the replacement.

TypedIO’s device handles and DMA objects serve as capabilities. vOSTD constructs them with private fields through discovery and OSTD-compatible DMA APIs; a bounded buffer range limits the bytes a request may access. When a request reaches Linux, the host checks the handle’s owning kernelet, object generation, access rights, and permitted use with that device.

Discovery checks the protocol identity/version, framework ABI version, and supported features. It requires exact agreement on the generated schemas of configuration, request, and reply types; their digests cover field types, variants, limits, target properties, and any directly copied layouts. The checked binding is retained in Queue<P>; submission obtains type information from Message, never from a caller-provided digest.

Opening reserves queue storage and completion credits, one reply slot for each request the queue can accept. It runs in a sleepable context, where the calling task may block while waiting for memory or locks. A credit is reserved before accepting a request so a full reply queue cannot block cleanup.

Proposed discovery and opening APIs.

/// An attachment authorized for the current kernelet.
pub struct Device<P: Protocol> { /* private capability and config */ }

/// Returns matching attachments after validating their schema and generation.
/// Unsupported schemas return an error rather than an unchecked typed device.
pub fn discover<P: Protocol>() -> Result<Vec<Device<P>>, DiscoverError>;

impl<P: Protocol> Device<P> {
    /// Returns the validated configuration snapshot for this attachment generation.
    pub fn config(&self) -> &P::Config;

    /// Opens a bounded queue with negotiated features and completion credits.
    /// May allocate and sleep; must not run under a spin-lock or interrupt-disable guard.
    pub fn open(&self, options: QueueOptions) -> Result<Queue<P>, OpenError>;
}

The block configuration supplies capacity, logical-block size (the required transfer unit), transfer limits, and read-only policy. Selecting a device does not bypass those limits: the host checks each submitted operation against the attachment.

DMA buffers

An asynchronous request needs device-accessible storage that remains allocated until the device finishes. OSTD already provides the public buffer abstractions for this purpose. TypedIO uses DmaCoherent and DmaStream<D> directly, adding trusted request retention inside vOSTD and the host framework.

Memory: DMA storage lifetime. Retaining or pinning pages keeps their storage available, whereas Rust Pin restricts object movement; neither guarantees exclusive access or device completion. A bounce buffer is separate storage used when direct device access is unsuitable. See Linux’s DMA guide and user-page pinning.

OSTD: DMA allocation and synchronization. DmaCoherent::alloc and DmaStream<D>::alloc allocate DMA storage. DmaStream<D>::map takes ownership of an existing USegment, a contiguous run of untyped frames: bytes that may change through userspace or devices, rather than typed kernel objects. Keeping this storage allocated prevents its reuse for typed objects.

Streaming buffers require sync_to_device before device reads and sync_from_device after device writes. Their sealed direction types—implementable only by OSTD—are ToDevice, FromDevice, and FromAndToDevice (the default); they restrict permitted CPU access and synchronization. The zeroing alloc constructor requires a CPU-writable direction, so it is unavailable for FromDevice; map can use that direction.

Both DMA types release their mappings on drop. Their byte-access APIs permit concurrent access without providing ordinary Rust references to the bytes. See the inspected coherent DMA API, streaming DMA API, slice API, and memory-access contract.

For vOSTD, the device side of these DMA abstractions is the TypedIO host service. Allocation or mapping prepares storage for that service; submission authorizes a particular request to access a particular range. A disk read needs host write access, corresponding to FromDevice; a disk write needs host read access, corresponding to ToDevice. Bidirectional DMA buffers support either operation, while coherent buffers have no direction parameter. The framework checks the operation’s access requirements against the buffer’s allowed directions before accepting work.

Asterinas: block-I/O requests. An Asterinas block-I/O request (BIO) groups an operation and its buffer segments. A BioSegment wraps a DMA-buffer range; a SubmittedBio owns the submitted request and reports its final status through complete. These are frontend objects, separate from Linux’s bio objects. See Asterinas’s BIO types.

The block protocol uses the existing bidirectional Slice<Arc<DmaStream>> found in BioSegment. Its Read and Write variants specify the host access required for each request. This preserves the current block interface without converting buffers into new direction-specific wrappers. Protocols with one-way buffers can instead use DmaStream<FromDevice> or DmaStream<ToDevice>.

Proposed frontend use of existing OSTD constructors. buffer is a USegment of at least 4 KiB; the selected vOSTD service accesses coherent memory. The request reuses the allocation; frontend is the initialized driver from the overview.

let dma = Arc::new(DmaStream::map(buffer, true)?);
let request = BlockRequest::Read {
    sector,
    buffers: [Slice::new(Arc::clone(&dma), 0..4096)].try_into()?,
};
let reply = wait_reply(frontend.submit(request)?)?;
if reply.status == BlockStatus::Success {
    dma.sync_from_device(0..4096)?;
    // Read the result through dma.reader().
}

A caller needing new storage can use DmaStream::alloc or, for a protocol accepting coherent buffers, DmaCoherent::alloc. A frontend with an existing BioSegment can reuse its DMA slice without allocating or mapping again. Slice::new checks its range and panics on invalid input; drivers must validate externally supplied ranges before calling it.

Transfer and request retention

A request retains its local DMA owner and its grant backing, the host allocation containing its bytes. The book’s grants supply the storage for kernelet frames. The lifetime section explains how these independent records survive early drop and instance death.

The proposed vOSTD DMA constructors register the prepared storage with the host framework and keep its identity private inside the DMA object. The Message support layer uses audited hooks to transfer that identity, range, and permitted access, retain the local owner, and construct a host-local buffer handle. Neither the guest’s Arc pointer nor its reference count or destructor crosses the boundary.

The initial DMA hooks grant request-scoped host access; they do not transfer DMA ownership between kernelets or import DMA owners in replies. Peer resource transfer supports host-owned pipe endpoints. Importing another instance’s DMA storage would need a separate lifetime and mapping contract.

Before dispatch, the host validates the owning instance, generation, range, direction, device authorization, and quota. The device address returned by daddr() is neither a host pointer nor sufficient authority. Rejection returns the original request and rolls back provisional retention.

This support is specific to the recognized DMA types, not a blanket implementation for Arc<T> or Slice<T>. The existing Slice lives in Asterinas’s aster-util library; its audited integration belongs in the shared message-support layer, using vOSTD’s resource hooks, rather than making vOSTD depend on the kernel library. Backends receive host-local counterparts with bounded byte-access operations, not guest DMA objects.

Synchronization and host access

The baseline is Asterinas/OSTD revision ab9a4cfdc726263b3f41ccea0337633e24b443fc, vOSTD’s documented API reference. Its DMA module and TypedIO retention hooks are proposed additions to vOSTD.

The initial vOSTD implementation uses coherent grant-backed storage: the CPU-visible and service-visible bytes are the same allocation. map retains an existing supported segment; alloc obtains supported storage. Unsupported backing is rejected during preparation, allowing the frontend to copy into a supported DMA buffer. The caller’s is_cache_coherent argument cannot authorize an unsafe mapping: vOSTD must establish the actual access properties. DMA preparation requires interrupts enabled under the existing OSTD contract; drivers prepare buffers before entering the non-sleeping submission path.

The frontend keeps the usual streaming synchronization sequence: sync_to_device before submitting a write, and sync_from_device after read completion and before CPU consumption. These calls need no cache maintenance for the initial coherent storage, but do not replace queue publication ordering or waiting for completion. Coherent buffers omit these calls. Neither API promises that other aliases have stopped accessing the bytes.

OSTD can also maintain separate CPU-visible and device-visible storage. Supporting that configuration later must preserve its synchronization semantics: the host accesses the registered device-visible storage, and the stream’s sync methods transfer data as required. Selecting the CPU-visible storage merely because it is easier to access would bypass that contract. A separate Linux-side bounce buffer is the host adapter’s responsibility. Its read copy-back must finish copying data into the registered DMA range before completion.

Compilers: opaque byte copies. Ordinary Rust loads and stores assume its aliasing and data-race rules hold. An opaque copy uses an assembly routine to access concurrently mutable bytes without creating Rust references to them or letting the compiler replace those accesses with ordinary loads and stores. This technique does not by itself establish compatibility with other concurrent access methods.

Reusing OSTD DMA types does not by itself make arbitrary Linux CPU accesses safe. TypedIO adds a trusted host byte-access adapter using bounded opaque copies compatible with vOSTD’s assembly-copy model. Safe host APIs expose copying operations, not slices into shared storage. Host parsers validate private snapshots of control data; mutable payload bytes never become unchecked addresses or allocation lengths. Cloned frames, userspace mappings, or pooled aliases may corrupt a faulty frontend’s I/O, but must not induce host Rust undefined behavior.

Direct hardware DMA is allowed only through an audited target whose entire data path preserves this access model; other targets use host-private bounce buffers. The Linux adapter performs any physical-device DMA mapping separately: vOSTD preparing a buffer for a TypedIO service does not prove that a particular Linux device can access it directly. Application memory initially enters through existing VmReader/VmWriter copying or owned untyped frames; neither an arbitrary userspace address nor a Vec<u8> becomes a DMA resource automatically.

Request submission and completion

A device queue must handle many requests without making the frontend wait for them in submission order. Queue::try_submit accepts a request and a frontend-chosen RequestTag; try_pop_completion returns whichever completed operation is next available. A completion identifies its request and carries either the protocol reply or a framework error. Each accepted request reserves completion space, so publication never depends on the frontend consuming another result first.

Proposed queue APIs. RequestId is an opaque framework identity containing the queue generation and a non-reused request sequence; RequestTag is a correlation value, not memory authority. The bitflags! macro defines a set that can contain several notification causes at once.

/// A bounded request/completion queue for one device attachment.
/// Created by `Device::open`; its protocol and attachment generation are fixed.
pub struct Queue<P: Protocol> { /* private queue and attachment state */ }

bitflags::bitflags! {
    /// Pending reasons to service a queue, returned by `Queue::take_events`.
    pub struct QueueEvents: u8 {
        /// One or more completions were published.
        /// Drain them with `try_pop_completion`; this flag is not a count.
        const COMPLETION_AVAILABLE = 1 << 0;

        /// Submission capacity changed from full to having room for a request.
        /// Retry `try_submit`; another submitter may have taken that capacity.
        const CAPACITY_AVAILABLE = 1 << 1;

        /// The attachment was removed and no longer accepts new requests.
        /// Accepted requests still require final completion after access ends.
        const REMOVED = 1 << 2;
    }
}

#[derive(Clone, Copy, PartialEq, Eq)]
pub struct RequestTag(pub u64);

pub struct Completion<R> {
    pub id: RequestId,
    pub tag: RequestTag,
    pub result: Result<R, RequestError>,
}

impl<P: Protocol> Queue<P> {
    /// Accepts an owned request, or returns it intact on rejection.
    /// Does not sleep or allocate; a full queue returns `WouldBlock`.
    pub fn try_submit(&self, request: P::Request, tag: RequestTag)
        -> Result<RequestId, SubmitError<P::Request>>;

    /// Removes one available completion, regardless of submission order.
    /// Returns `None` when no completion is currently available.
    pub fn try_pop_completion(&self) -> Option<Completion<P::Reply>>;

    /// Requests cancellation without establishing that buffer access has ended.
    pub fn cancel(&self, id: RequestId) -> Result<(), CancelError>;
}

Event flags are latched: each remains set until take_events atomically returns and clears it, and repeated occurrences coalesce into one bit. Taking events neither removes completion records nor clears the attachment’s permanent removed state. The IRQ methods use these flags to coordinate draining and notification delivery.
